DBSCAN Clustering\n\n## Overview\nDBSCAN (Density-Based Spatial Clustering of Applications with Noise) is a density-based clustering algorithm that groups together points that are closely packed together, marking points in low-density regions as outliers.\n\n## Installation\n```bash\npip install scikit-learn\n```\n\n## Basic Usage\n\n### Standard DBSCAN with Euclidean Distance\n```python\nfrom sklearn.cluster import DBSCAN\nimport numpy as np\n\n# Sample data: array of shape (n_samples, n_features)\nX = np.array([[1, 2], [2, 2], [2, 3], [8, 7], [8, 8], [25, 80]])\n\n# Create DBSCAN instance\ndb = DBSCAN(eps=3, min_samples=2)\n\n# Fit the model\nlabels = db.fit_predict(X)\n\n# labels: array of cluster assignments (-1 for noise/outliers)\n# Example: [0, 0, 0, 1, 1, -1]\n```\n\n### Custom Distance Metric\nDBSCAN supports custom distance metrics through the `metric` parameter:\n\n```python\nfrom sklearn.cluster import DBSCAN\n\n# Using a callable custom metric\ndef custom_distance(a, b, shape_weight=1.0):\n    \"\"\"\n    Custom weighted distance metric.\n\n    Args:\n        a, b: Points to compare (numpy arrays)\n        shape_weight: Weight parameter (w)\n\n    Returns:\n        Distance between points\n    \"\"\"\n    dx = a[0] - b[0]\n    dy = a[1] - b[1]\n    w = shape_weight\n    return np.sqrt((w * dx)**2 + ((2 - w) * dy)**2)\n\n# For metrics with parameters, use functools.partial\nfrom functools import partial\n\nmetric_fn = partial(custom_distance, shape_weight=1.2)\ndb = DBSCAN(eps=10, min_samples=3, metric=metric_fn)\nlabels = db.fit_predict(X)\n```\n\n### Using Precomputed Distance Matrix\nFor more control over distance calculations:\n\n```python\nfrom sklearn.cluster import DBSCAN\nfrom scipy.spatial.distance import pdist, squareform\nimport numpy as np\n\n# Compute pairwise distances with custom function\ndef custom_distance_matrix(X, shape_weight):\n    n = len(X)\n    distances = np.zeros((n, n))\n    for i in range(n):\n        for j in range(i+1, n):\n            dx = X[i, 0] - X[j, 0]\n            dy = X[i, 1] - X[j, 1]\n            dist = np.sqrt((shape_weight * dx)**2 + ((2 - shape_weight) * dy)**2)\n            distances[i, j] = dist\n            distances[j, i] = dist\n    return distances\n\n# Use precomputed metric\ndist_matrix = custom_distance_matrix(X, shape_weight=1.2)\ndb = DBSCAN(eps=10, min_samples=3, metric='precomputed')\nlabels = db.fit_predict(dist_matrix)\n```\n\n## Parameters\n\n### eps (epsilon)\n- Maximum distance between two samples to be considered in the same neighborhood\n- Critical parameter that controls cluster granularity\n- Smaller values create more, smaller clusters\n- Larger values merge clusters together\n\n### min_samples\n- Minimum number of samples in a neighborhood for a point to be considered a core point\n- Higher values require denser regions to form clusters\n- Points in regions with fewer than min_samples neighbors are marked as noise (-1)\n\n## Output\n\n### Cluster Labels\n- `labels_`: Array of cluster assignments after fitting\n- Values ≥ 0: cluster ID\n- Value = -1: noise/outlier point\n\n### Computing Cluster Centroids\n```python\n# Get cluster labels (excluding noise)\nunique_labels = set(labels)\nif -1 in unique_labels:\n    unique_labels.remove(-1)\n\ncentroids = []\nfor label in unique_labels:\n    # Get all points in this cluster\n    cluster_points = X[labels == label]\n    # Compute centroid as mean position\n    centroid = cluster_points.mean(axis=0)\n    centroids.append(centroid)\n\ncentroids = np.array(centroids)\n```\n\n## Common Use Cases\n- Spatial data clustering (geographic coordinates, image pixels)\n- Anomaly detection (noise points)\n- Finding clusters of arbitrary shape\n- Clustering without knowing number of clusters beforehand\n\n## Advantages\n- No need to specify number of clusters\n- Can find arbitrarily shaped clusters\n- Robust to outliers\n- Works well with spatial data\n\n## Limitations\n- Sensitive to epsilon parameter selection\n- Struggles with varying density clusters\n- O(n²) time complexity without {"type":"assistant","message":{"model":"claude-sonnet-4-5-20250929","id":"msg_vrtx_01R9w3M5VDPk6hiPSqL5A4n9","type":"message","role":"assistant","content":[{"type":"tool_use","id":"toolu_vrtx_017MApfwNpr1fjJpzhaZ495c","name":"Write","input":{"file_path":"/root/environment/skills/pareto-frontier.md","content":"# Pareto Frontier Optimization\n\n## Overview\nA Pareto frontier (also called Pareto front or Pareto optimal set) represents the set of solutions where no objective can be improved without worsening another objective. In multi-objective optimization, these are the \"non-dominated\" solutions.\n\n## Concept\n\n### Pareto Dominance\nSolution A **dominates** solution B if:\n- A is better than or equal to B in all objectives, AND\n- A is strictly better than B in at least one objective\n\nA solution is **Pareto optimal** if no other solution dominates it.\n\n### Example\nOptimizing two objectives: maximize F1 score, minimize delta\n\n```\nSolution A: F1=0.8, delta=10\nSolution B: F1=0.7, delta=8\nSolution C: F1=0.9, delta=15\nSolution D: F1=0.8, delta=12\n```\n\nAnalysis:\n- A dominates B (higher F1, lower delta)\n- A dominates D (same F1, lower delta)\n- Neither A nor C dominates the other (A has lower delta, C has higher F1)\n- Pareto frontier: {A, C}\n\n## Implementation in Python\n\n### Method 1: Pairwise Comparison\n```python\nimport numpy as np\n\ndef is_pareto_optimal(costs, maximize_mask=None):\n    \"\"\"\n    Find Pareto optimal points.\n\n    Args:\n        costs: 2D array where each row is a solution, columns are objectives\n               Assumes minimization for all objectives by default\n        maximize_mask: Boolean array indicating which objectives to maximize\n                       If None, all objectives are minimized\n\n    Returns:\n        Boolean array indicating which solutions are Pareto optimal\n    \"\"\"\n    costs = np.array(costs)\n    n_points = costs.shape[0]\n\n    # Convert maximization objectives to minimization\n    if maximize_mask is not None:\n        costs = costs.copy()\n        costs[:, maximize_mask] = -costs[:, maximize_mask]\n\n    is_optimal = np.ones(n_points, dtype=bool)\n\n    for i in range(n_points):\n        if is_optimal[i]:\n            # Check if any other point dominates point i\n            # Point j dominates i if: all(j <= i) and any(j < i)\n            dominated = np.all(costs <= costs[i], axis=1) & \\\n                       np.any(costs < costs[i], axis=1)\n            dominated[i] = False  # Point doesn't dominate itself\n\n            if np.any(dominated):\n                is_optimal[i] = False\n\n    return is_optimal\n\n\n# Example usage\ndata = np.array([\n    [0.8, 10],   # F1, delta\n    [0.7, 8],\n    [0.9, 15],\n    [0.8, 12]\n])\n\n# F1: maximize (index 0), delta: minimize (index 1)\nmaximize_mask = np.array([True, False])\npareto_optimal = is_pareto_optimal(data, maximize_mask)\npareto_frontier = data[pareto_optimal]\n\nprint(\"Pareto optimal solutions:\")\nprint(pareto_frontier)\n```\n\n### Method 2: Efficient Implementation\nFor large datasets, use a more efficient algorithm:\n\n```python\ndef find_pareto_frontier_efficient(costs, maximize_mask=None):\n    \"\"\"\n    Efficiently find Pareto frontier points.\n\n    Args:\n        costs: 2D numpy array (n_solutions, n_objectives)\n        maximize_mask: Boolean array for objectives to maximize\n\n    Returns:\n        Boolean mask of Pareto optimal points\n    \"\"\"\n    costs = np.array(costs, dtype=float)\n    n_points = costs.shape[0]\n\n    # Convert maximization to minimization\n    if maximize_mask is not None:\n        costs = costs.copy()\n        costs[:, maximize_mask] = -costs[:, maximize_mask]\n\n    # Sort by first objective\n    sorted_indices = np.argsort(costs[:, 0])\n    sorted_costs = costs[sorted_indices]\n\n    pareto_mask = np.ones(n_points, dtype=bool)\n\n    for i in range(n_points):\n        if pareto_mask[sorted_indices[i]]:\n            # Check if dominated by any previous Pareto point\n            for j in range(i):\n                if pareto_mask[sorted_indices[j]]:\n                    # Check if j dominates i\n                    if np.all(sorted_costs[j] <= sorted_costs[i]) and \\\n                       np.any(sorted_costs[j] < sorted_costs[i]):\n                        pareto_mask[sorted_indices[i]] = False\n                        break\n\n    return pareto_mask\n```\n\n### Extracting Results\n```python\nimport pandas as pd\n\n# Assuming you have results in a DataFrame\nresults_df = pd.DataFrame({\n    'F1': [0.8, 0.7, 0.9, 0.8],\n    'delta': [10, 8, 15, 12],\n    'param1': [3, 4, 3, 5],\n    'param2': [0.5, 0.6, 0.7, 0.5]\n})\n\n# Create cost matrix\ncosts = results_df[['F1', 'delta']].values\n\n# Find Pareto frontier (maximize F1, minimize delta)\nmaximize_mask = np.array([True, False])\npareto_mask = is_pareto_optimal(costs, maximize_mask)\n\n# Extract Pareto optimal solutions\npareto_df = results_df[pareto_mask]\n\nprint(f\"Total solutions: {len(results_df)}\")\nprint(f\"Pareto optimal solutions: {len(pareto_df)}\")\nprint(pareto_df)\n```\n\n## Visualization\n```python\nimport matplotlib.pyplot as plt\n\n# Plot all solutions\nplt.figure(figsize=(10, 6))\nplt.scatter(results_df['F1'], results_df['delta'],\n           alpha=0.5, label='All solutions', s=50)\n\n# Highlight Pareto frontier\npareto_points = results_df[pareto_mask]\nplt.scatter(pareto_points['F1'], pareto_points['delta'],\n           color='red', label='Pareto frontier', s=100, marker='*')\n\n# Draw Pareto frontier line\npareto_sorted = pareto_points.sort_values('F1')\nplt.plot(pareto_sorted['F1'], pareto_sorted['delta'],\n        'r--', alpha=0.5)\n\nplt.xlabel('F1 Score (maximize)')\nplt.ylabel('Delta (minimize)')\nplt.title('Pareto Frontier')\nplt.legend()\nplt.grid(True, alpha=0.3)\nplt.show()\n```\n\n## Common Applications\n- Hyperparameter optimization (accuracy vs. speed)\n- Resource allocation (cost vs. performance)\n- Multi-criteria decision making\n- Engineering design optimization\n- Portfolio optimization\n\n## Tips\n- Pre-filter solutions with hard constraints (e.g., F1 > 0.5) before finding Pareto frontier\n- Normalize objectives if they have very different scales\n- For many objectives (>3), consider using specialized algorithms (NSGA-II, SPEA2)\n- Pareto frontiers can be visualized in 2D/3D but become harder to interpret with more objectives\n"}}],"stop_reason":null,"stop_sequence":null,"usage":{"input_tokens":5,"cache_creation_input_tokens":149,"cache_read_input_tokens":49399,"cache_creation":{"ephemeral_5m_input_tokens":149,"ephemeral_1h_input_tokens":0},"output_tokens":1},"context_management":null},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"19d08fff-f5da-4912-aa3c-c00ed584d224","uuid":"ecf3fe0e-f2ef-40e8-a308-337e416738e1"}
{"type":"assistant","message":{"model":"claude-sonnet-4-5-20250929","id":"msg_vrtx_01R9w3M5VDPk6hiPSqL5A4n9","type":"message","role":"assistant","content":[{"type":"tool_use","id":"toolu_vrtx_017C6rAM4b6EpCwLpfwNyfB1","name":"Write","input":{"file_path":"/root/environment/skills/f1-score-calculation.md","content":"# F1 Score for Clustering Evaluation\n\n## Overview\nF1 score is a metric that balances precision and recall. In clustering contexts, it measures how well predicted clusters match ground truth labels.\n\n## Formula\n```\nF1 = 2 * (Precision * Recall) / (Precision + Recall)\n```\n\nWhere:\n- **Precision**: What fraction of predicted positives are actually positive\n- **Recall**: What fraction of actual positives were correctly predicted\n\nRange: 0.0 (worst) to 1.0 (best)\n\n## Application to Cluster Matching\n\n### Set-Based Matching\nWhen matching predicted clusters to ground truth points:\n\n```python\nimport numpy as np\n\ndef compute_f1_for_matching(predicted_centroids, ground_truth_points,\n                           matches, max_distance=100):\n    \"\"\"\n    Compute F1 score for cluster matching.\n\n    Args:\n        predicted_centroids: Array of predicted cluster centers\n        ground_truth_points: Array of ground truth points\n        matches: List of (pred_idx, gt_idx) tuples for matched pairs\n        max_distance: Maximum distance for valid match\n\n    Returns:\n        F1 score\n    \"\"\"\n    n_predicted = len(predicted_centroids)\n    n_ground_truth = len(ground_truth_points)\n    n_matches = len(matches)\n\n    # True Positives: number of matched pairs\n    true_positives = n_matches\n\n    # False Positives: predicted clusters without a match\n    false_positives = n_predicted - n_matches\n\n    # False Negatives: ground truth points without a match\n    false_negatives = n_ground_truth - n_matches\n\n    # Compute precision and recall\n    if true_positives + false_positives == 0:\n        precision = 0.0\n    else:\n        precision = true_positives / (true_positives + false_positives)\n\n    if true_positives + false_negatives == 0:\n        recall = 0.0\n    else:\n        recall = true_positives / (true_positives + false_negatives)\n\n    # Compute F1\n    if precision + recall == 0:\n        f1 = 0.0\n    else:\n        f1 = 2 * (precision * recall) / (precision + recall)\n\n    return f1\n\n\n# Example usage\npredicted = np.array([[10, 20], [50, 60], [90, 100]])\nground_truth = np.array([[12, 22], [88, 98]])\n\n# Matches: [(0, 0), (2, 1)] means:\n# - predicted[0] matches ground_truth[0]\n# - predicted[2] matches ground_truth[1]\n# - predicted[1] has no match\nmatches = [(0, 0), (2, 1)]\n\nf1 = compute_f1_for_matching(predicted, ground_truth, matches)\nprint(f\"F1 score: {f1:.4f}\")\n\n# Analysis:\n# TP = 2 (two matches)\n# FP = 1 (predicted[1] unmatched)\n# FN = 0 (all ground truth matched)\n# Precision = 2/3 = 0.667\n# Recall = 2/2 = 1.0\n# F1 = 2 * (0.667 * 1.0) / (0.667 + 1.0) = 0.800\n```\n\n## Edge Cases\n\n### No Clusters Found\n```python\n# If DBSCAN finds no clusters (all noise)\npredicted_centroids = np.array([])  # Empty\nground_truth_points = np.array([[10, 20], [30, 40]])\n\nn_matches = 0\nTP = 0\nFP = 0\nFN = len(ground_truth_points)  # All ground truth unmatched\n\n# Recall = 0 / (0 + 2) = 0\n# Precision undefined (0/0), but typically set to 0\n# F1 = 0\n```\n\n### No Ground Truth\n```python\n# If there are no ground truth points for an image\npredicted_centroids = np.array([[10, 20]])\nground_truth_points = np.array([])  # Empty\n\nn_matches = 0\nTP = 0\nFP = len(predicted_centroids)\nFN = 0\n\n# Precision = 0 / (0 + 1) = 0\n# Recall undefined (0/0), but typically set to 0\n# F1 = 0\n```\n\n### Perfect Match\n```python\n# All predicted clusters match all ground truth\npredicted = np.array([[10, 20], [50, 60]])\nground_truth = np.array([[11, 21], [51, 61]])\nmatches = [(0, 0), (1, 1)]\n\nTP = 2\nFP = 0\nFN = 0\nPrecision = 2/2 = 1.0\nRecall = 2/2 = 1.0\nF1 = 1.0\n```\n\n## Averaging F1 Across Multiple Images\n\n```python\ndef average_f1_scores(f1_scores):\n    \"\"\"\n    Average F1 scores across multiple images.\n\n    Args:\n        f1_scores: List of F1 scores (including 0.0 for failed cases)\n\n    Returns:\n        Average F1 score\n    \"\"\"\n    # Include all F1 values (including 0.0)\n    # This properly penalizes images where clustering failed\n    return np.mean(f1_scores)\n\n\n# Example\nf1_per_image = [0.8, 0.9, 0.0, 0.75, 0.0, 0.85]  # Some images failed\navg_f1 = average_f1_scores(f1_per_image)\nprint(f\"Average F1: {avg_f1:.4f}\")  # 0.55\n```\n\n## Using scikit-learn\n\nFor binary classification problems:\n```python\nfrom sklearn.metrics import f1_score, precision_score, recall_score\n\ny_true = [0, 1, 1, 0, 1, 1]\ny_pred = [0, 1, 0, 0, 1, 1]\n\nf1 = f1_score(y_true, y_pred)\nprecision = precision_score(y_true, y_pred)\nrecall = recall_score(y_true, y_pred)\n\nprint(f\"Precision: {precision:.3f}\")\nprint(f\"Recall: {recall:.3f}\")\nprint(f\"F1: {f1:.3f}\")\n```\n\n## Interpretation\n\n### High F1 (> 0.8)\n- Good balance of precision and recall\n- Most predicted clusters match ground truth\n- Few false positives and false negatives\n\n### Medium F1 (0.5 - 0.8)\n- Moderate performance\n- Some mismatch between predicted and ground truth\n- May indicate need for parameter tuning\n\n### Low F1 (< 0.5)\n- Poor performance\n- Many unmatched predictions or missing ground truth matches\n- Significant parameter adjustment needed\n\n## Related Metrics\n\n### Precision\n- Focus on minimizing false positives\n- \"Of the clusters I found, how many are real?\"\n\n### Recall\n- Focus on minimizing false negatives\n- \"Of the real clusters, how many did I find?\"\n\n### F-beta Score\n```python\ndef f_beta_score(precision, recall, beta=1.0):\n    \"\"\"\n    Generalized F score with beta parameter.\n    beta > 1: weights recall higher\n    beta < 1: weights precision higher\n    beta = 1: F1 score (balanced)\n    \"\"\"\n    if precision + recall == 0:\n        return 0.0\n    return (1 + beta**2) * (precision * recall) / (beta**2 * precision + recall)\n```\n"}}],"stop_reason":null,"stop_sequence":null,"usage":{"input_tokens":5,"cache_creation_input_tokens":149,"cache_read_input_tokens":49399,"cache_creation":{"ephemeral_5m_input_tokens":149,"ephemeral_1h_input_tokens":0},"output_tokens":1},"context_management":null},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"19d08fff-f5da-4912-aa3c-c00ed584d224","uuid":"3f27c174-d674-4555-b0fc-992d45ba97af"}
{"type":"assistant","message":{"model":"claude-sonnet-4-5-20250929","id":"msg_vrtx_01R9w3M5VDPk6hiPSqL5A4n9","type":"message","role":"assistant","content":[{"type":"tool_use","id":"toolu_vrtx_018cadkcW9txTEYTCwmHppsZ","name":"Write","input":{"file_path":"/root/environment/skills/greedy-matching.md","content":"# Greedy Matching Algorithm\n\n## Overview\nGreedy matching iteratively selects the best available match at each step without reconsidering previous choices. For point matching, this typically means matching the closest pair first, then the next closest, and so on.\n\n## Algorithm\n\n### Basic Greedy Matching\n```python\nimport numpy as np\nfrom scipy.spatial.distance import cdist\n\ndef greedy_match_points(set1, set2, max_distance=None):\n    \"\"\"\n    Match points from set1 to set2 using greedy closest-pair approach.\n\n    Args:\n        set1: Array of shape (n1, 2) - first set of points\n        set2: Array of shape (n2, 2) - second set of points\n        max_distance: Maximum allowed distance for a valid match (optional)\n\n    Returns:\n        List of tuples (idx1, idx2, distance) for matched pairs\n    \"\"\"\n    if len(set1) == 0 or len(set2) == 0:\n        return []\n\n    # Compute all pairwise distances\n    distances = cdist(set1, set2, metric='euclidean')\n\n    matches = []\n    used_set1 = set()\n    used_set2 = set()\n\n    # Flatten distance matrix and sort by distance\n    n1, n2 = distances.shape\n    dist_list = []\n    for i in range(n1):\n        for j in range(n2):\n            dist_list.append((i, j, distances[i, j]))\n\n    dist_list.sort(key=lambda x: x[2])  # Sort by distance\n\n    # Greedily select matches\n    for idx1, idx2, dist in dist_list:\n        # Skip if either point already matched\n        if idx1 in used_set1 or idx2 in used_set2:\n            continue\n\n        # Skip if distance exceeds threshold\n        if max_distance is not None and dist > max_distance:\n            continue\n\n        # Accept this match\n        matches.append((idx1, idx2, dist))\n        used_set1.add(idx1)\n        used_set2.add(idx2)\n\n    return matches\n\n\n# Example usage\npredicted_centroids = np.array([\n    [10, 20],\n    [50, 60],\n    [90, 95]\n])\n\nground_truth = np.array([\n    [12, 22],\n    [91, 94],\n    [200, 200]  # Far away\n])\n\nmatches = greedy_match_points(predicted_centroids, ground_truth,\n                              max_distance=100)\n\nfor idx1, idx2, dist in matches:\n    print(f\"Predicted[{idx1}] {predicted_centroids[idx1]} -> \"\n          f\"GT[{idx2}] {ground_truth[idx2]}, distance={dist:.2f}\")\n\n# Output:\n# Predicted[0] [10 20] -> GT[0] [12 22], distance=2.83\n# Predicted[2] [90 95] -> GT[1] [91 94], distance=1.41\n# Note: GT[2] at [200,200] is too far (>100) so not matched\n```\n\n## Properties\n\n### Advantages\n- Simple and fast: O(n² log n) for sorting, O(n²) overall\n- Deterministic: same input always gives same output\n- Intuitive: matches closest pairs first\n- Works well when matches are clearly separated\n\n### Disadvantages\n- Not globally optimal: may miss better overall matching\n- Greedy choices can block better later matches\n- No backtracking: early mistakes propagate\n\n### When to Use\n- Point matching in spatial data\n- Simple correspondence problems\n- When speed is important\n- When local optimal is good enough\n\n## Comparison with Optimal Matching\n\n### Hungarian Algorithm (Optimal)\n```python\nfrom scipy.optimize import linear_sum_assignment\n\ndef optimal_match_points(set1, set2, max_distance=None):\n    \"\"\"\n    Find optimal bipartite matching using Hungarian algorithm.\n\n    Minimizes total distance across all matches.\n    \"\"\"\n    if len(set1) == 0 or len(set2) == 0:\n        return []\n\n    distances = cdist(set1, set2, metric='euclidean')\n\n    # Apply max_distance constraint\n    if max_distance is not None:\n        distances = distances.copy()\n        distances[distances > max_distance] = 1e9  # Large penalty\n\n    # Find optimal assignment\n    row_ind, col_ind = linear_sum_assignment(distances)\n\n    matches = []\n    for i, j in zip(row_ind, col_ind):\n        dist = cdist([set1[i]], [set2[j]])[0, 0]\n        if max_distance is None or dist <= max_distance:\n            matches.append((i, j, dist))\n\n    return matches\n```\n\n### Example Showing Difference\n```python\n# Set up problem where greedy is suboptimal\nset1 = np.array([[0, 0], [10, 0]])\nset2 = np.array([[1, 0], [9, 0]])\n\n# Greedy matching:\n# Closest pair: set1[0] to set2[0], distance=1\n# Next: set1[1] to set2[1], distance=1\n# Total distance: 2\n\ngreedy_matches = greedy_match_points(set1, set2)\ngreedy_total = sum(m[2] for m in greedy_matches)\nprint(f\"Greedy total distance: {greedy_total:.2f}\")\n\n# Optimal matching (same in this case):\n# set1[0] to set2[0], distance=1\n# set1[1] to set2[1], distance=1\n# Total distance: 2\n\noptimal_matches = optimal_match_points(set1, set2)\noptimal_total = sum(m[2] for m in optimal_matches)\nprint(f\"Optimal total distance: {optimal_total:.2f}\")\n\n# Example where greedy differs:\nset1 = np.array([[0, 0], [5, 0]])\nset2 = np.array([[4, 0], [11, 0]])\n\n# Greedy: [0->0, 1->1] = 4+6=10\n# Optimal: [0->0, 1->1] = 4+6=10 (same)\n# But with different positions:\n\nset1 = np.array([[0, 0], [5, 0]])\nset2 = np.array([[4.5, 0], [5.5, 0]])\n\n# Greedy: [1->0, 0->1] = 0.5+5.5=6 (picks closest first)\n# Optimal: [0->0, 1->1] = 4.5+0.5=5 (better total)\n```\n\n## Variations\n\n### One-to-Many Matching\nAllow multiple points from set1 to match same point in set2:\n\n```python\ndef greedy_match_one_to_many(set1, set2, max_distance=None):\n    \"\"\"Each point in set1 matches to at most one in set2,\n    but set2 points can have multiple matches.\"\"\"\n\n    matches = []\n    used_set1 = set()\n\n    distances = cdist(set1, set2, metric='euclidean')\n\n    dist_list = []\n    for i in range(len(set1)):\n        for j in range(len(set2)):\n            dist_list.append((i, j, distances[i, j]))\n\n    dist_list.sort(key=lambda x: x[2])\n\n    for idx1, idx2, dist in dist_list:\n        if idx1 in used_set1:\n            continue\n        if max_distance is not None and dist > max_distance:\n            continue\n\n        matches.append((idx1, idx2, dist))\n        used_set1.add(idx1)  # Only mark set1 as used\n\n    return matches\n```\n\n### K-Nearest Neighbors Match\nMatch each point to its k nearest neighbors:\n\n```python\ndef knn_match(set1, set2, k=1, max_distance=None):\n    \"\"\"Match each point in set1 to k nearest in set2.\"\"\"\n\n    distances = cdist(set1, set2, metric='euclidean')\n\n    matches = []\n    for i in range(len(set1)):\n        # Get k nearest neighbors\n        sorted_indices = np.argsort(distances[i])[:k]\n        for j in sorted_indices:\n            dist = distances[i, j]\n            if max_distance is None or dist <= max_distance:\n                matches.append((i, j, dist))\n\n    return matches\n```\n\n## Performance Tips\n\n### Pre-filter by Distance\n```python\n# For large datasets, filter before sorting\ndist_list = [(i, j, distances[i, j])\n             for i in range(n1)\n             for j in range(n2)\n             if max_distance is None or distances[i, j] <= max_distance]\n```\n\n### Use Spatial Indexing\nFor very large datasets, use KD-trees:\n```python\nfrom scipy.spatial import cKDTree\n\ndef fast_greedy_match(set1, set2, max_distance):\n    \"\"\"Fast greedy matching using KD-tree.\"\"\"\n    tree = cKDTree(set2)\n\n    matches = []\n    used_set2 = set()\n\n    # For each point, find candidates within max_distance\n    candidates = []\n    for i, point in enumerate(set1):\n        indices = tree.query_ball_point(point, max_distance)\n        for j in indices:\n            dist = np.linalg.norm(point - set2[j])\n            candidates.append((i, j, dist))\n\n    candidates.sort(key=lambda x: x[2])\n\n    used_set1 = set()\n    for idx1, idx2, dist in candidates:\n        if idx1 not in used_set1 and idx2 not in used_set2:\n            matches.append((idx1, idx2, dist))\n            used_set1.add(idx1)\n            used_set2.add(idx2)\n\n    return matches\n```\n"}}],"stop_reason":null,"stop_sequence":null,"usage":{"input_tokens":5,"cache_creation_input_tokens":149,"cache_read_input_tokens":49399,"cache_creation":{"ephemeral_5m_input_tokens":149,"ephemeral_1h_input_tokens":0},"output_tokens":1},"context_management":null},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"19d08fff-f5da-4912-aa3c-c00ed584d224","uuid":"0f1433c0-e2eb-4d02-8e94-402b603fa7c2"}

The best choice depends on whether the task is CPU-bound or I/O-bound.\n\n## Multiprocessing for CPU-Bound Tasks\n\n### Basic Pool\n```python\nfrom multiprocessing import Pool\nimport numpy as np\n\ndef process_item(item):\n    \"\"\"Function to process a single item.\"\"\"\n    # DBSCAN Clustering\n\n## Overview\nDBSCAN (Density-Based Spatial Clustering of Applications with Noise) is a density-based clustering algorithm that groups together points that are closely packed together, marking points in low-density regions as outliers.\n\n## Installation\n```bash\npip install scikit-learn\n```\n\n## Basic Usage\n\n### Standard DBSCAN with Euclidean Distance\n```python\nfrom sklearn.cluster import DBSCAN\nimport numpy as np\n\n# Sample data: array of shape (n_samples, n_features)\nX = np.array([[1, 2], [2, 2], [2, 3], [8, 7], [8, 8], [25, 80]])\n\n# Create DBSCAN instance\ndb = DBSCAN(eps=3, min_samples=2)\n\n# Fit the model\nlabels = db.fit_predict(X)\n\n# labels: array of cluster assignments (-1 for noise/outliers)\n# Example: [0, 0, 0, 1, 1, -1]\n```\n\n### Custom Distance Metric\nDBSCAN supports custom distance metrics through the `metric` parameter:\n\n```python\nfrom sklearn.cluster import DBSCAN\n\n# Using a callable custom metric\ndef custom_distance(a, b, shape_weight=1.0):\n    \"\"\"\n    Custom weighted distance metric.\n\n    Args:\n        a, b: Points to compare (numpy arrays)\n        shape_weight: Weight parameter (w)\n\n    Returns:\n        Distance between points\n    \"\"\"\n    dx = a[0] - b[0]\n    dy = a[1] - b[1]\n    w = shape_weight\n    return np.sqrt((w * dx)**2 + ((2 - w) * dy)**2)\n\n# For metrics with parameters, use functools.partial\nfrom functools import partial\n\nmetric_fn = partial(custom_distance, shape_weight=1.2)\ndb = DBSCAN(eps=10, min_samples=3, metric=metric_fn)\nlabels = db.fit_predict(X)\n```\n\n### Using Precomputed Distance Matrix\nFor more control over distance calculations:\n\n```python\nfrom sklearn.cluster import DBSCAN\nfrom scipy.spatial.distance import pdist, squareform\nimport numpy as np\n\n# Compute pairwise distances with custom function\ndef custom_distance_matrix(X, shape_weight):\n    n = len(X)\n    distances = np.zeros((n, n))\n    for i in range(n):\n        for j in range(i+1, n):\n            dx = X[i, 0] - X[j, 0]\n            dy = X[i, 1] - X[j, 1]\n            dist = np.sqrt((shape_weight * dx)**2 + ((2 - shape_weight) * dy)**2)\n            distances[i, j] = dist\n            distances[j, i] = dist\n    return distances\n\n# Use precomputed metric\ndist_matrix = custom_distance_matrix(X, shape_weight=1.2)\ndb = DBSCAN(eps=10, min_samples=3, metric='precomputed')\nlabels = db.fit_predict(dist_matrix)\n```\n\n## Parameters\n\n### eps (epsilon)\n- Maximum distance between two samples to be considered in the same neighborhood\n- Critical parameter that controls cluster granularity\n- Smaller values create more, smaller clusters\n- Larger values merge clusters together\n\n### min_samples\n- Minimum number of samples in a neighborhood for a point to be considered a core point\n- Higher values require denser regions to form clusters\n- Points in regions with fewer than min_samples neighbors are marked as noise (-1)\n\n## Output\n\n### Cluster Labels\n- `labels_`: Array of cluster assignments after fitting\n- Values ≥ 0: cluster ID\n- Value = -1: noise/outlier centroids.append(centroid)\n\ncentroids = np.array(centroids)\n```\n\n## Common Use Cases\n- Spatial data clustering (geographic coordinates, image pixels)\n- Anomaly detection (noise points)\n- In multi-objective optimization, these are the \"non-dominated\" solutions.\n\n## Concept\n\n### Pareto Dominance\nSolution A **dominates** solution B if:\n- A is better than or equal to B in all objectives, AND\n- A is strictly better than B in at least one objective\n\nA solution is **Pareto optimal** if no other solution dominates it.\n\n### Example\nOptimizing two objectives: maximize F1 score, minimize delta\n\n```\nSolution A: F1=0.8, delta=10\nSolution B: F1=0.7, delta=8\nSolution C: F1=0.9, delta=15\nSolution D: F1=0.8, delta=12\n```\n\nAnalysis:\n- A dominates B (higher F1, lower delta)\n- A dominates D (same F1, lower delta)\n- Neither A nor C dominates the other (A has lower delta, C has higher F1)\n- Pareto frontier: {A, C}\n\n## Implementation in Python\n\n### Method 1: Pairwise Comparison\n```python\nimport numpy as np\n\ndef is_pareto_optimal(costs, maximize_mask=None):\n    \"\"\"\n    Find Pareto optimal points.\n\n    Args:\n        costs: 2D array where each row is a solution, columns are objectives\n               Assumes minimization for all objectives by default\n        maximize_mask: Boolean array indicating which objectives to maximize\n                       If None, all objectives are minimized\n\n    Returns:\n        Boolean array indicating which solutions are Pareto optimal\n    \"\"\"\n    costs = np.array(costs)\n    n_points = costs.shape[0]\n\n    # Convert maximization objectives to minimization\n    if maximize_mask is not None:\n        costs = costs.copy()\n        costs[:, maximize_mask] = -costs[:, maximize_mask]\n\n    is_optimal = np.ones(n_points, dtype=bool)\n\n    for i in range(n_points):\n        if is_optimal[i]:\n            # Check if any other point dominates point i\n            # Point j dominates i if: all(j <= i) and any(j < i)\n            dominated = np.all(costs <= costs[i], axis=1) & \\\n                       np.any(costs < costs[i], axis=1)\n            dominated[i] = False  # Point doesn't dominate itself\n\n            if np.any(dominated):\n                is_optimal[i] = False\n\n    return is_optimal\n\n\n# Example usage\ndata = np.array([\n    [0.8, 10],   # F1, delta\n    [0.7, 8],\n    [0.9, 15],\n    [0.8, 12]\n])\n\n# F1: maximize (index 0), delta: minimize (index 1)\nmaximize_mask = np.array([True, False])\npareto_optimal = is_pareto_optimal(data, maximize_mask)\npareto_frontier = data[pareto_optimal]\n\nprint(\"Pareto optimal solutions:\")\nprint(pareto_frontier)\n```\n\n### Method 2: Efficient Implementation\nFor large datasets, use a more efficient algorithm:\n\n```python\ndef find_pareto_frontier_efficient(costs, maximize_mask=None):\n    \"\"\"\n    Efficiently find Pareto frontier points.\n\n    Args:\n        costs: 2D numpy array (n_solutions, n_objectives)\n        maximize_mask: Boolean array for objectives to maximize\n\n    Returns:\n        Boolean mask of Pareto optimal points\n    \"\"\"\n    costs = np.array(costs, dtype=float)\n    n_points = costs.shape[0]\n\n    # Convert maximization to minimization\n    if maximize_mask is not None:\n        costs = costs.copy()\n        costs[:, maximize_mask] = -costs[:, maximize_mask]\n\n    # Sort by first objective\n    sorted_indices = np.argsort(costs[:, 0])\n    sorted_costs = costs[sorted_indices]\n\n    pareto_mask = np.ones(n_points, dtype=bool)\n\n    for i in range(n_points):\n        if pareto_mask[sorted_indices[i]]:\n            # Check if dominated by any previous Pareto point\n            for j in range(i):\n                if pareto_mask[sorted_indices[j]]:\n                    # Check if j dominates i\n                    if np.all(sorted_costs[j] <= sorted_costs[i]) and \\\n                       np.any(sorted_costs[j] < sorted_costs[i]):\n                        pareto_mask[sorted_indices[i]] = False\n                        break\n\n    return pareto_mask\n```\n\n### Extracting Results\n```python\nimport pandas as pd\n\n# Assuming you have results in a DataFrame\nresults_df = pd.DataFrame({\n    'F1': [0.8, 0.7, 0.9, 0.8],\n    'delta': [10, 8, 15, 12],\n    'param1': [3, 4, 3, 5],\n    'param2': [0.5, 0.6, 0.7, 0.5]\n})\n\n# Create cost matrix\ncosts = results_df[['F1', 'delta']].values\n\n# Find Pareto frontier (maximize F1, minimize delta)\nmaximize_mask = np.array([True, False])\npareto_mask = is_pareto_optimal(costs, maximize_mask)\n\n# Extract Pareto optimal solutions\npareto_df = results_df[pareto_mask]\n\nprint(f\"Total solutions: {len(results_df)}\")\nprint(f\"Pareto optimal solutions: {len(pareto_df)}\")\nprint(pareto_df)\n```\n\n## Visualization\n```python\nimport matplotlib.pyplot as plt\n\n# Plot all solutions\nplt.figure(figsize=(10, 6))\nplt.scatter(results_df['F1'], In clustering contexts, it measures how well predicted clusters match ground truth labels.\n\n## Formula\n```\nF1 = 2 * (Precision * Recall) / (Precision + Recall)\n```\n\nWhere:\n- **Precision**: What fraction of predicted positives are actually positive\n- **Recall**: What fraction of actual positives were correctly predicted\n\nRange: 0.0 (worst) to 1.0 (best)\n\n## Application to Cluster Matching\n\n### Set-Based Matching\nWhen matching predicted clusters to ground truth points:\n\n```python\nimport numpy as np\n\ndef compute_f1_for_matching(predicted_centroids, ground_truth_points,\n                           matches, max_distance=100):\n    \"\"\"\n    Compute F1 score for cluster matching.\n\n    Args:\n        predicted_centroids: Array of predicted cluster centers\n        ground_truth_points: Array of ground truth points\n        matches: List of (pred_idx, gt_idx) tuples for matched pairs\n        max_distance: Maximum distance for valid match\n\n    Returns:\n        F1 score\n    \"\"\"\n    n_predicted = len(predicted_centroids)\n    n_ground_truth = len(ground_truth_points)\n    n_matches = len(matches)\n\n    # True Positives: number of matched pairs\n    true_positives = n_matches\n\n    # False Positives: predicted clusters without a match\n    false_positives = n_predicted - n_matches\n\n    # False Negatives: ground truth points without a match\n    false_negatives = n_ground_truth - n_matches\n\n    # Compute precision and recall\n    if true_positives + false_positives == 0:\n        precision = 0.0\n    else:\n        precision = true_positives / (true_positives + false_positives)\n\n    if true_positives + false_negatives == 0:\n        recall = 0.0\n    else:\n        recall = true_positives / (true_positives + false_negatives)\n\n    # Compute F1\n    if precision + recall == 0:\n        f1 = 0.0\n    else:\n        f1 = 2 * (precision * recall) / (precision + recall)\n\n    return f1\n\n\n# Example usage\npredicted = np.array([[10, 20], [50, 60], [90, 100]])\nground_truth = np.array([[12, 22], [88, 98]])\n\n# Matches: [(0, 0), (2, 1)] means:\n# - predicted[0] matches ground_truth[0]\n# - predicted[2] matches ground_truth[1]\n# - predicted[1] has no match\nmatches = [(0, 0), (2, 1)]\n\nf1 = compute_f1_for_matching(predicted, ground_truth, matches)\nprint(f\"F1 score: {f1:.4f}\")\n\n# Analysis:\n# TP = 2 (two matches)\n# FP = 1 (predicted[1] unmatched)\n# FN = 0 (all ground truth matched)\n# Precision = 2/3 = 0.667\n# Recall = 2/2 = 1.0\n# F1 = 2 * (0.667 * 1.0) / (0.667 + 1.0) = 0.800\n```\n\n## Edge Cases\n\n### No Clusters Found\n```python\n# If DBSCAN finds no clusters (all noise)\npredicted_centroids = np.array([])  # Empty\nground_truth_points = np.array([[10, 20], [30, 40]])\n\nn_matches = 0\nTP = 0\nFP = 0\nFN = len(ground_truth_points)  # All ground truth unmatched\n\n# Recall = 0 / (0 + 2) = 0\n# Precision undefined (0/0), but typically set to 0\n# F1 = 0\n```\n\n### No Ground Truth\n```python\n# If there are no ground truth points for an image\npredicted_centroids = np.array([[10, 20]])\nground_truth_points = np.array([])  # Empty\n\nn_matches = 0\nTP = 0\nFP = len(predicted_centroids)\nFN = 0\n\n# Precision = 0 / (0 + 1) = 0\n# Recall undefined (0/0), but typically set to 0\n# F1 = 0\n```\n\n### Perfect Match\n```python\n# All predicted clusters match all ground truth\npredicted = np.array([[10, 20], [50, 60]])\nground_truth = np.array([[11, 21], [51, 61]])\nmatches = [(0, 0), (1, 1)]\n\nTP = 2\nFP = 0\nFN = 0\nPrecision = 2/2 = 1.0\nRecall = 2/2 = 1.0\nF1 = 1.0\n```\n\n## Averaging F1 Across Multiple Images\n\n```python\ndef average_f1_scores(f1_scores):\n    \"\"\"\n    Average F1 scores across multiple images.\n\n    Args:\n        f1_scores: List of F1 scores (including 0.0 for failed cases)\n\n    Returns:\n        Average F1 score\n    \"\"\"\n    # Include all F1 values (including 0.0)\n    # This properly penalizes images where clustering failed\n    return np.mean(f1_scores)\n\n\n# Example\nf1_per_image = [0.8, 0.9, 0.0, 0.75, 0.0, 0.85]  # Some images failed\navg_f1 = average_f1_scores(f1_per_image)\nprint(f\"Average F1: {avg_f1:.4f}\")  # 0.55\n```\n\n## Using scikit-learn\n\nFor binary classification problems:\n```python\nfrom sklearn.metrics import f1_score, precision_score, recall_score\n\ny_true = [0, 1, 1, 0, 1, 1]\ny_pred = [0, 1, 0, 0, 1, 1]\n\nf1 = f1_score(y_true, y_pred)\nprecision = precision_score(y_true, y_pred)\nrecall = recall_score(y_true, y_pred)\n\nprint(f\"Precision: {precision:.3f}\")\nprint(f\"Recall: {recall:.3f}\")\nprint(f\"F1: {f1:.3f}\")\n```\n\n## Interpretation\n\n### High F1 (> 0.8)\n- Good balance of precision and recall\n- Most predicted clusters match ground truth\n- Few false positives and false negatives\n\n### Medium F1 (0.5 - 0.8)\n- Moderate performance\n- Some mismatch between predicted and ground truth\n- May indicate need for parameter tuning\n\n### Low F1 (< 0.5)\n- Poor performance\n- Many unmatched predictions or missing ground truth matches\n- Significant parameter adjustment needed\n\n## Related Metrics\n\n### Precision\n- Focus on minimizing false positives\n- \"Of the clusters I found, how many are real?\"\n\n### Recall\n- Focus on minimizing false negatives\n- \"Of the real clusters, how many did I find?\"\n\n### F-beta Score\n```python\ndef f_beta_score(precision, recall, beta=1.0):\n    \"\"\"\n    Generalized F score with beta parameter.\n    beta > 1: weights recall higher\n    beta < 1: weights precision higher\n    beta = 1: F1 score (balanced)\n    \"\"\"\n    if precision + recall == 0:\n        return 0.0\n    return (1 + beta**2) * (precision * recall) / (beta**2 * precision + recall)\n```\n","structuredPatch":[],"originalFile":null}}

For point matching, this typically means matching the closest pair first, then the next closest, and so on.\n\n## Algorithm\n\n### Basic Greedy Matching\n```python\nimport numpy as np\nfrom scipy.spatial.distance import cdist\n\ndef greedy_match_points(set1, set2, max_distance=None):\n    \"\"\"\n    Match points from set1 to set2 using greedy closest-pair approach.\n\n    Args:\n        set1: Array of shape (n1, 2) - first set of points\n        set2: Array of shape (n2, 2) - second set of points\n        max_distance: Maximum allowed distance for a valid match (optional)\n\n    Returns:\n        List of tuples (idx1, idx2, distance) for matched pairs\n    \"\"\"\n    if len(set1) == 0 or len(set2) == 0:\n        return []\n\n    # Compute all pairwise distances\n    distances = cdist(set1, set2, metric='euclidean')\n\n    matches = []\n    used_set1 = set()\n    used_set2 = set()\n\n    # Flatten distance matrix and sort by distance\n    n1, n2 = distances.shape\n    dist_list = []\n    for i in range(n1):\n        for j in range(n2):\n            dist_list.append((i, j, distances[i, j]))\n\n    dist_list.sort(key=lambda x: x[2])  # Sort by distance\n\n    # Greedily select matches\n    for idx1, idx2, dist in dist_list:\n        # Skip if either point already matched\n        if idx1 in used_set1 or idx2 in used_set2:\n            continue\n\n        # Skip if distance exceeds threshold\n        if max_distance is not None and dist > max_distance:\n            continue\n\n        # Accept this match\n        matches.append((idx1, idx2, dist))\n        used_set1.add(idx1)\n        used_set2.add(idx2)\n\n    return matches\n\n\n# Example usage\npredicted_centroids = np.array([\n    [10, 20],\n    [50, 60],\n    [90, 95]\n])\n\nground_truth = np.array([\n    [12, 22],\n    [91, 94],\n    [200, 200]  # Far away\n])\n\nmatches = greedy_match_points(predicted_centroids, ground_truth,\n                              max_distance=100)\n\nfor idx1, idx2, dist in matches:\n    print(f\"Predicted[{idx1}] {predicted_centroids[idx1]} -> \"\n          f\"GT[{idx2}] {ground_truth[idx2]}, distance={dist:.2f}\")\n\n# Output:\n# Predicted[0] [10 20] -> GT[0] [12 22], distance=2.83\n# Predicted[2] [90 95] -> GT[1] [91 94], distance=1.41\n# Note: GT[2] at [200,200] is too far (>100) so not matched\n```\n\n## Properties\n\n### Advantages\n- Simple and fast: O(n² log n) for sorting, O(n²) overall\n- Deterministic: same input always gives same output\n- Intuitive: matches closest pairs first\n- Works well when matches are clearly separated\n\n### Disadvantages\n- Not globally optimal: may miss better overall matching\n- Greedy choices can block better later matches\n- No backtracking: early mistakes propagate\n\n### When to Use\n- Point matching in spatial data\n- Simple correspondence problems\n- When speed is important\n- When local optimal is good enough\n\n## Comparison with Optimal Matching\n\n### Hungarian Algorithm (Optimal)\n```python\nfrom scipy.optimize import linear_sum_assignment\n\ndef optimal_match_points(set1, set2, max_distance=None):\n    \"\"\"\n    Find optimal bipartite matching using Hungarian algorithm.\n\n    Minimizes total distance across all matches.\n    \"\"\"\n    if len(set1) == 0 or len(set2) == 0:\n        return []\n\n    distances = cdist(set1, set2, metric='euclidean')\n\n    # Apply max_distance constraint\n    if max_distance is not None:\n        distances = distances.copy()\n        distances[distances > max_distance] = 1e9  # Large penalty\n\n    # Find optimal assignment\n    row_ind, col_ind = linear_sum_assignment(distances)\n\n    matches = []\n    for i, j in zip(row_ind, col_ind):\n        dist = cdist([set1[i]], [set2[j]])[0, 0]\n        if max_distance is None or dist <= max_distance:\n            matches.append((i, j, dist))\n\n    return matches\n```\n\n### Example Showing Difference\n```python\n# Set up problem where greedy is suboptimal\nset1 = np.array([[0, 0], [10, 0]])\nset2 = np.array([[1, 0], [9, 0]])\n\n# Greedy matching:\n# Closest pair: set1[0] to set2[0], distance=1\n# Next: set1[1] to set2[1], distance=1\n# Total distance: 2\n\ngreedy_matches = greedy_match_points(set1, set2)\ngreedy_total = sum(m[2] for m in greedy_matches)\nprint(f\"Greedy total distance: {greedy_total:.2f}\")\n\n# Optimal matching (same in this case):\n# set1[0] to set2[0], distance=1\n# set1[1] to set2[1], distance=1\n# Total distance: 2\n\noptimal_matches = optimal_match_points(set1, set2)\noptimal_total = sum(m[2] for m in optimal_matches)\nprint(f\"Optimal total distance: {optimal_total:.2f}\")\n\n# Example where greedy differs:\nset1 = np.array([[0, 0], [5, 0]])\nset2 = np.array([[4, 0], [11, 0]])\n\n# Greedy: [0->0, 1->1] = 4+6=10\n# Optimal: [0->0, 1->1] = 4+6=10 (same)\n# But with different positions:\n\nset1 = np.array([[0, 0], [5, 0]])\nset2 = np.array([[4.5, 0], [5.5, 0]])\n\n# Greedy: [1->0, 0->1] = 0.5+5.5=6 (picks closest first)\n# Optimal: [0->0, 1->1] = 4.5+0.5=5 (better total)\n```\n\n## Variations\n\n### One-to-Many Matching\nAllow multiple points from set1 to match same point in set2:\n\n```python\ndef greedy_match_one_to_many(set1, set2, max_distance=None):\n    \"\"\"Each point in set1 matches to at most one in set2,\n    but set2 points can have multiple matches.\"\"\"\n\n    matches = []\n    used_set1 = set()\n\n    distances = cdist(set1, set2, metric='euclidean')\n\n    dist_list = []\n    for i in range(len(set1)):\n        for j in range(len(set2)):\n            dist_list.append((i, j, distances[i, j]))\n\n    dist_list.sort(key=lambda x: x[2])\n\n    for idx1, idx2, dist in dist_list:\n        if idx1 in used_set1:\n            continue\n        if max_distance is not None and dist > max_distance:\n            continue\n\n        matches.append((idx1, idx2, dist))\n        used_set1.add(idx1)  # Only mark set1 as used\n\n    return matches\n```\n\n### K-Nearest Neighbors Match\nMatch each point to its k nearest neighbors:\n\n```python\ndef knn_match(set1, set2, k=1, max_distance=None):\n    \"\"\"Match each point in set1 to k nearest in set2.\"\"\"\n\n    distances = cdist(set1, set2, metric='euclidean')\n\n    matches = []\n    for i in range(len(set1)):\n        # Get k nearest neighbors\n        sorted_indices = np.argsort(distances[i])[:k]\n        for j in sorted_indices:\n            dist = distances[i, j]\n            if max_distance is None or dist <= max_distance:\n                matches.append((i, j, dist))\n\n    return matches\n```\n\n## Performance Tips\n\n### Pre-filter by Distance\n```python\n# For large datasets, filter before sorting\ndist_list = [(i, j, distances[i, j])\n             for i in range(n1)\n             for j in range(n2)\n             if max_distance is None or distances[i, j] <= max_distance]\n```\n\n### Use Spatial Indexing\nFor very large datasets, use KD-trees:\n```python\nfrom scipy.spatial import cKDTree\n\ndef fast_greedy_match(set1, set2, max_distance):\n    \"\"\"Fast greedy matching using KD-tree.\"\"\"\n    tree = cKDTree(set2)\n\n    matches = []\n    used_set2 = set()\n\n    # For each point, find candidates within max_distance\n    candidates = []\n    for i, point in enumerate(set1):\n        indices = tree.query_ball_point(point, max_distance)\n        for j in indices:\n            dist = np.linalg.norm(point - set2[j])\n            candidates.append((i, j, dist))\n\n    candidates.sort(key=lambda x: x[2])\n\n    used_set1 = set()\n    for idx1, idx2, dist in candidates:\n        if idx1 not in used_set1 and idx2 not in used_set2:\n            matches.append((idx1, idx2, dist))\n            used_set1.add(idx1)\n            used_set2.add(idx2)\n\n    return matches\n```\n","structuredPatch":[],"originalFile":null}}

The best choice depends on whether the task is CPU-bound or I/O-bound.\n\n## Multiprocessing for CPU-Bound Tasks\n\n### Basic Pool\n```python\nfrom multiprocessing import Pool\nimport numpy as np\n\ndef process_item(item):\n    \"\"\"Function to process a single item.\"\"\"\n    # CPU-intensive computation\n    result = item ** 2\n    return result\n\n# Create pool of worker processes\nif __name__ == '__main__':\n    data = list(range(100))\n\n    # Use all available CPUs\n    with Pool() as pool:\n        results = pool.map(process_item, data)\n\n    print(results)\n```\n\n### Parallel Grid Search\n```python\nfrom multiprocessing import Pool\nfrom itertools import product\n\ndef evaluate_params(params):\n    \"\"\"Evaluate a single parameter combination.\"\"\"\n    min_samples, epsilon, shape_weight = params\n\n    # Run computation\n    score = some_expensive_function(min_samples, epsilon, shape_weight)\n\n    return {\n        'min_samples': min_samples,\n        'epsilon': epsilon,\n        'shape_weight': shape_weight,\n        'score': score\n    }\n\nif __name__ == '__main__':\n    # Create parameter grid\n    min_samples_range = range(3, 10)  # 3-9\n    epsilon_range = range(4, 26, 2)   # 4, 6, 8, ..., 24\n    shape_weight_range = np.arange(0.9, 2.0, 0.1)  # 0.9-1.9\n\n    param_grid = list(product(min_samples_range,\n                             epsilon_range,\n                             shape_weight_range))\n\n    print(f\"Total combinations: {len(param_grid)}\")\n\n    # Process in parallel\n    with Pool() as pool:\n        results = pool.map(evaluate_params, param_grid)\n\n    # Results is a list of dictionaries\n    print(f\"Completed {len(results)} evaluations\")\n```\n\n### With Progress Tracking\n```python\nfrom multiprocessing import Pool\nfrom tqdm import tqdm\n\ndef worker_func(x):\n    # Simulate work\n    import time\n    time.sleep(0.1)\n    return x ** 2\n\nif __name__ == '__main__':\n    data = range(100)\n\n    # Use imap for progress tracking\n    with Pool() as pool:\n        results = list(tqdm(pool.imap(worker_func, data),\n                           total=len(data),\n                           desc=\"Processing\"))\n```\n\n### Specifying Number of Workers\n```python\nimport multiprocessing\n\n# Get number of CPUs\nn_cpus = multiprocessing.cpu_count()\nprint(f\"Available CPUs: {n_cpus}\")\n\n# Use specific number of workers\nwith Pool(processes=4) as pool:\n    results = pool.map(func, data)\n\n# Common strategy: leave one CPU free\nwith Pool(processes=n_cpus - 1) as pool:\n    results = pool.map(func, data)\n```\n\n## Chunking Large Tasks\n\n### Manual Chunking\n```python\ndef process_chunk(chunk_data):\n    \"\"\"Process a chunk of data.\"\"\"\n    chunk_id, items = chunk_data\n    results = []\n    for item in items:\n        result = expensive_computation(item)\n        results.append(result)\n    return results\n\nif __name__ == '__main__':\n    data = list(range(1000))\n    n_chunks = 10\n    chunk_size = len(data) // n_chunks\n\n    # Create chunks\n    chunks = [(i, data[i*chunk_size:(i+1)*chunk_size])\n              for i in range(n_chunks)]\n\n    with Pool() as pool:\n        chunk_results = pool.map(process_chunk, chunks)\n\n    # Flatten results\n    results = [item for chunk in chunk_results for item in chunk]\n```\n\n### Automatic Chunking with chunksize\n```python\n# Pool.map has automatic chunking\nwith Pool() as pool:\n    # Process in chunks of 10\n    results = pool.map(func, data, chunksize=10)\n```\n\n## JobLib (Recommended for ML Tasks)\n\n### Installation\n```bash\npip install joblib\n```\n\n### Basic Usage\n```python\nfrom joblib import Parallel, delayed\n\ndef process_item(item):\n    return item ** 2\n\n# Parallel processing with joblib\nresults = Parallel(n_jobs=-1)(delayed(process_item)(item)\n                              for item in range(100))\n\n# n_jobs=-1 uses all CPUs\n# n_jobs=4 uses 4 CPUs\n```\n\n### With Progress Bar\n```python\nfrom joblib import Parallel, delayed\n\nresults = Parallel(n_jobs=-1, verbose=10)(\n    delayed(process_item)(item) for item in range(100)\n)\n\n# verbose levels:\n# 0: no output\n# 10: prints progress\n# 50: prints more details\n```\n\n### Backend Selection\n```python\n# Use threads for I/O-bound tasks\nresults = Parallel(n_jobs=4, backend='threading')(\n    delayed(io_task)(item) for item in data\n)\n\n# Use processes for CPU-bound tasks (default)\nresults = Parallel(n_jobs=4, backend='multiprocessing')(\n    delayed(cpu_task)(item) for item in data\n)\n\n# Use loky (more robust, default in newer versions)\nresults = Parallel(n_jobs=4, backend='loky')(\n    delayed(task)(item) for item in data\n)\n```\n\n## Best Practices\n\n### Memory Management\n```python\n# Return only necessary data\ndef efficient_worker(params):\n    # Do heavy computation\n    heavy_result = expensive_computation(params)\n\n    # Return only summary, not full data\n    return {\n        'params': params,\n        'score': heavy_result.mean(),  # Not entire array\n        'count': len(heavy_result)\n    }\n```\n\n### Error Handling\n```python\ndef safe_worker(item):\n    try:\n        result = risky_computation(item)\n        return {'success': True, 'result': result, 'item': item}\n    except Exception as e:\n        return {'success': False, 'error': str(e), 'item': item}\n\nresults = Parallel(n_jobs=-1)(delayed(safe_worker)(item)\n                              for item in data)\n\n# Process results\nsuccesses = [r for r in results if r['success']]\nfailures = [r for r in results if not r['success']]\n\nprint(f\"Succeeded: {len(successes)}, Failed: {len(failures)}\")\n```\n\n### Shared Data with Multiprocessing\n```python\nfrom multiprocessing import Pool, Manager\n\ndef worker(args):\n    idx, shared_dict = args\n    # Access shared data (read-only is faster)\n    value = shared_dict['data'][idx]\n    return value ** 2\n\nif __name__ == '__main__':\n    manager = Manager()\n    shared_dict = manager.dict()\n    shared_dict['data'] = list(range(100))\n\n    args = [(i, shared_dict) for i in range(100)]\n\n    with Pool() as pool:\n        results = pool.map(worker, args)\n```\n\n## Performance Comparison\n\n### Overhead Considerations\n```python\nimport time\n\ndef small_task(x):\n    return x ** 2\n\ndef large_task(x):\n    # Simulate heavy computation\n    return sum(i**2 for i in range(10000))\n\n# Small tasks: overhead dominates\nstart = time.time()\nresults = [small_task(i) for i in range(100)]\nserial_time = time.time() - start\n\nstart = time.time()\nwith Pool() as pool:\n    results = pool.map(small_task, range(100))\nparallel_time = time.time() - start\n\nprint(f\"Serial: {serial_time:.4f}s, Parallel: {parallel_time:.4f}s\")\n# Parallel may be slower due to overhead!\n\n# Large tasks: parallelization wins\nstart = time.time()\nresults = [large_task(i) for i in range(100)]\nserial_time = time.time() - start\n\nstart = time.time()\nwith Pool() as pool:\n    results = pool.map(large_task, range(100))\nparallel_time = time.time() - start\n\nprint(f\"Serial: {serial_time:.4f}s, Parallel: {parallel_time:.4f}s\")\n# Parallel should be much faster\n```\n\n## Common Pitfalls\n\n### 1. Forgetting `if __name__ == '__main__'`\n```python\n# WRONG - will cause issues on Windows\nwith Pool() as pool:\n    results = pool.map(func, data)\n\n# CORRECT\nif __name__ == '__main__':\n    with Pool() as pool:\n        results = pool.map(func, data)\n```\n\n### 2. Lambda Functions\n```python\n# WRONG - lambdas can't be pickled\nwith Pool() as pool:\n    results = pool.map(lambda x: x**2, data)\n\n# CORRECT - use named function\ndef square(x):\n    return x ** 2\n\nwith Pool() as pool:\n    results = pool.map(square, data)\n```\n\n### 3. Global Variables\n```python\n# WRONG - globals not shared between processes\nglobal_data = []\n\ndef worker(x):\n    global_data.append(x)  # Won't work as expected\n    return x\n\n# CORRECT - return data or use Manager\ndef worker(x):\n    return x  # Return and collect results\n\nwith Pool() as pool:\n    results = pool.map(worker, data)\n```\n","structuredPatch":[],"originalFile":null}}

X: Array of points (n_points, 2)\n        shape_weight: Weight parameter (w)\n\n    Returns:\n        Distance matrix (n_points, n_points)\n    \"\"\"\n    n = len(X)\n    distances = np.zeros((n, points from set1 to set2 using greedy closest-pair approach.\n    Uses standard Euclidean distance for matching.\n\n    Args:\n        set1: Array of shape (n1, 2) - first set of points (e.g., idx2, distance) for matched pairs\n    \"\"\"\n    if len(set1) == 0 or len(set2) == 0:\n        return []\n\n    # Compute all pairwise distances using standard Euclidean\n    distances = cdist(set1, set2, metric='euclidean')\n\n    matches = []\n    used_set1 = set()\n    used_set2 = set()\n\n    # Flatten distance matrix and sort by distance\n    n1, n2 = distances.shape\n    dist_list = []\n compute_f1_for_matching(n_predicted, n_ground_truth, n_matches)\n\n    # Compute delta (average distance of matched pairs)\n    if n_matches == 0:\n        delta = np.nan\n    else:\n        # Extract distances from matches (already in standard Euclidean)\n        delta = np.mean([dist for _, _, dist in matches])\n\n    return (f1, delta)\n\n\ndef evaluate_params(params_data):\n    \"\"\"\n    Evaluate a single parameter combination across all images.\n\n    Args:\n        params_data: Tuple of (min_samples, epsilon, shape_weight, image_data_list, all_expert_file_rads)\n\n    Returns:\n        Dictionary with parameters and performance metrics\n    \"\"\"\n    min_samples, epsilon, shape_weight, image_data_list, all_expert_file_rads = params_data\n\n    f1_scores = []\n    deltas = []\n\n    # Loop over all unique images from expert dataset\n    for file_rad in all_expert_file_rads:\n        if file_rad in image_data_list:\n            citsci_points, expert_points = image_data_list[file_rad]\n        else:\n            # No citizen science data for this image\n            citsci_points = np.array([]).reshape(0, 2)\n            # We still need expert points for this image\n            expert_points = expert_df[expert_df['file_rad'] == file_rad][['x', 'y']].values\n\n        f1, delta = evaluate_image((citsci_points, expert_points),\n                                  min_samples, epsilon, shape_weight)\n        f1_scores.append(f1)\n        deltas.append(delta)\n\n    # Average F1 across all images (including 0.0 values)\n    avg_f1 = np.mean(f1_scores)\n\n    # Average delta excluding NaN values\n    deltas_valid = [d for d in deltas if not np.isnan(d)]\n    if len(deltas_valid) > 0:\n        avg_delta = np.mean(deltas_valid)\n    else:\n        avg_delta = np.nan\n\n    return {\n        'F1': avg_f1,\n        'delta': avg_delta,\n        'min_samples': min_samples,\n        'epsilon': epsilon,\n        'shape_weight': shape_weight\n    }\n\n\ndef is_pareto_optimal(costs, maximize_mask=None):\n    \"\"\"\n    Find Pareto optimal points.\n\n    Args:\n        costs: 2D array where each row is a solution, columns are objectives\n        maximize_mask: Boolean array indicating which objectives to maximize\n\n    Returns:\n        Boolean array indicating which solutions are Pareto optimal\n    \"\"\"\n    costs = np.array(costs)\n    n_points = costs.shape[0]\n\n    # Convert maximization objectives to minimization\n    if maximize_mask is not None:\n        costs = costs.copy()\n        costs[:, maximize_mask] = -costs[:, maximize_mask]\n\n    is_optimal = np.ones(n_points, dtype=bool)\n\n    for i in range(n_points):\n        if is_optimal[i]:\n            # Check if any other point dominates point i\n            # Point j dominates i if: all(j <= i) and any(j < i)\n            dominated = np.all(costs <= costs[i], axis=1) & \\\n                       np.any(costs < costs[i], axis=1)\n            dominated[i] = False  # Point doesn't dominate itself\n\n            if np.any(dominated):\n                is_optimal[i] = False\n\n    return is_optimal\n\n\nif __name__ == '__main__':\n    print(\"Loading data...\")\n    # Load data\n    citsci_df = pd.read_csv('/root/data/citsci_train.csv')\n    expert_df = pd.read_csv('/root/data/expert_train.csv')\n\n    print(f\"Citizen science points: {len(citsci_df)}\")\n    print(f\"Expert points: {len(expert_df)}\")\n\n    # Group by file_rad\n    citsci_grouped = citsci_df.groupby('file_rad')\n    expert_grouped = expert_df.groupby('file_rad')\n\n    # Get all unique file_rads from expert dataset\n    all_expert_file_rads = sorted(expert_df['file_rad'].unique())\n    print(f\"Unique images in expert dataset: {len(all_expert_file_rads)}\")\n\n    # Prepare image data\n    image_data_list = {}\n    for file_rad in all_expert_file_rads:\n        # Get citizen science points for this image\n        if file_rad in citsci_grouped.groups:\n            citsci_points = citsci_grouped.get_group(file_rad)[['x', 'y']].values\n        else:\n            citsci_points = np.array([]).reshape(0, 2)\n\n        # Get expert points for this image\n        expert_points = expert_grouped.get_group(file_rad)[['x', 'y']].values\n\n        image_data_list[file_rad] = (citsci_points, expert_points)\n\n epsilon_range = range(4, 26, 2)   # 4, 6, 8, ..., 24\n    shape_weight_range = np.arange(0.9, 2.0, 0.1)  # 0.9-1.9\n\n    param_grid = list(product(min_samples_range,\n                             epsilon_range,\n                             shape_weight_range))\n\n    print(f\"Total parameter combinations: {len(param_grid)}\")\n\n    # Prepare data for parallel processing\n    params_data_list results = Parallel(n_jobs=-1, verbose=10)(\n        delayed(evaluate_params)(params_data) for params_data in params_data_list\n    )\n\n    # Convert to DataFrame\n    results_df = pd.DataFrame(results)\n\n    print(f\"\\nTotal results: {len(results_df)}\")\n    print(f\"Results with valid delta: {results_df['delta'].notna().sum()}\")\n\n    # Filter by F1 > 0.5\n    filtered_df = results_df[results_df['F1'] > 0.5].copy()\n    print(f\"Results with F1 > 0.5: {len(filtered_df)}\")\n\n    if len(filtered_df) == 0:\n        print(\"WARNING: No results with F1 > 0.5 found!\")\n print(\"ERROR: No valid results for Pareto frontier!\")\n        exit(1)\n\n    # Create cost matrix for Pareto analysis\n    costs = filtered_valid[['F1', 'delta']].values\n\n    # Find Pareto frontier (maximize F1, minimize delta)\n    maximize_mask = np.array([True, False])\n    pareto_mask = is_pareto_optimal(costs, maximize_mask)\n\n    # Extract Pareto optimal solutions\n    pareto_df = filtered_valid[pareto_mask].copy()\n\n    print(f\"\\nPareto frontier size: {len(pareto_df)}\")\n\n    # Round values\n    pareto_df['F1'] = pareto_df['F1'].round(5)\n    pareto_df['delta'] =
